Permissive evidence-research notebook and handoff:
Research pass completed. I did not assign scores, ranges, anchors, or ranking advice.

The workspace is read-only, so I could not write the supplied `research-materials.md` or `research-ledger-manifest.json` paths. The complete handoff is below.

## Reconnaissance

Tshisekedi was president throughout 2022, having taken office on 24 January 2019. The official constitutional materials identify presidential responsibility for the Council of Ministers, senior appointments, and armed-forces command; practical authority was constrained by inherited institutions, coalition politics, weak administrative capacity, and donor dependence. The official presidential biography confirms his office and tenure; the constitutional text supplies the formal authority baseline. ([Presidency biography](https://www.presidence.cd/president/le-president), [DRC Constitution](https://www.presidence.cd/uploads/files/Constitution%20de%20la%20RDC%202011.pdf))

The information environment was mixed: journalists and citizens could openly criticize officials, but intimidation, arbitrary detention, restrictive defamation/security laws, and state-of-siege enforcement narrowed safe reporting. The U.S. State Department recorded 43 freedom-of-expression abuses and 15 assembly abuses documented by UNJHRO in the first half of 2022. Internet access was not generally disrupted, although monitoring without adequate legal authority was reported. ([2022 human-rights report](https://www.ecoi.net/de/dokument/2089109.html))

The dominant repeated event was eastern insecurity: M23, ADF, CODECO, other armed groups, FARDC conduct, displacement, the state of siege, and regional peace processes. This creates strong exposure but also attribution and visibility risks. The relevant denominators include approximately 1.78 million displaced people in Ituri and millions of displaced or out-of-school children. ([U.S. State Department report](https://www.ecoi.net/de/dokument/2089109.html), [UNICEF/ECW](https://www.unicef.org/drcongo/en/press-releases/call-substantial-increase-funding-crisis-affected-children))

| E006 | U.S. State Department: journalists and defenders were arrested or threatened, especially in state-of-siege provinces; criticism could trigger detention. | Target-period political-freedom and safety evidence; 3B.1–3B.5, 3B.8, 4B.3, 4B.6, 4B.9. ([source](https://www.ecoi.net/de/dokument/2089109.html)) |
| E007 | U.S. State Department: peaceful assembly was restricted; security forces sometimes used deadly force; UNJHRO documented 15 abuses of assembly rights. | Target-period negative evidence; 3B.8–3B.9, 4B.3, 4B.9. ([source](https://www.ecoi.net/de/dokument/2089109.html)) |
| E008 | U.S. State Department: gubernatorial elections were largely peaceful and observers were accredited, but allegations of vote-buying implicated Tshisekedi’s coalition. | Mixed electoral evidence; 4B.1–4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.10. The vote-buying allegation remains an allegation. ([source](https://www.ecoi.net/de/dokument/2089109.html)) |
| E009 | Freedom House: civil society alleged Tshisekedi’s CENI restructuring preserved presidential influence over the electoral body. | Adverse institutional evidence; 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.8. This is principally 2021 baseline evidence, not a standalone 2022 act. ([source](https://freedomhouse.org/country/democratic-republic-congo/freedom-world/2022)) |
| E010 | African Union: eastern insecurity involved multiple armed groups; Luanda and Nairobi processes, regional forces, dialogue, ceasefire, and de-escalation were supported. | International-security and diplomacy evidence; 2B.1, 2B.2, 2B.4, 2B.5, 2B.7, 2B.10. ([source](https://www.peaceau.org/en/article/communique-of-the-1103rd-meeting-of-the-peace-and-security-council-held-on-31-august-2022-on-the-situation-in-the-eastern-democratic-republic-of-congo-drc)) |
| E011 | EAC: Kenyatta consulted Tshisekedi in November 2022; the Nairobi dialogue incorporated Congolese stakeholders and was coordinated with the Luanda process. | Direct diplomatic-process evidence; 2B.1, 2B.7, 2B.10, 8B.3, 8B.9. | E012 | UN Secretary-General statement: Luanda summit produced a ceasefire and proposed M23 withdrawal, with MONUSCO support for implementation. | Peace-process evidence; 2B.1, 2B.7, 2B.10. | E013 | Presidential address: Tshisekedi characterized M23 as externally supported and framed the conflict around sovereignty and mineral interests. | Official threat justification; 2B.3, 2B.6, 2B.10. Requires independent corroboration; retained as an official claim, not established fact. ([source](https://presidence.cd/actualite-detail/actualite/adresse_a_la_nation_du_president_de_la_republique_felix_tshisekedi_suite_a_la_deterioration_de_la_situation_securitaire_a_lest_du_pays)) |
| E014 | IMF July 2022 report: authorities halted central-bank financing, pursued macroeconomic reforms, and were advised to strengthen governance and natural-wealth management. | Policy/action evidence with institutional attribution; 5B.3–5B.7, 5B.10, 8B.2, 8B.6–8B.8. ([source](https://www.imf.org/en/publications/cr/issues/2022/07/05/democratic-republic-of-the-congo-staff-report-for-the-2022-article-iv-consultation-second-520400)) |
| E015 | IMF November 2022 review: reform program continued; projected growth was 6.6%, with inflation above 12%; policies addressed buffers, inflation, and structural reform. | Target-period implementation/outcome evidence, with external-shock caveats; 5B.3, 5B.9–5B.10, 8B.6–8B.8. ([source](https://www.imf.org/en/news/articles/2022/11/18/pr22395-democratic-republic-congo-imf-staff-reach-staff-level-agreement-third-review-under-ecf)) |
| E016 | UNICEF: government and education partners worked on the 2022–23 school year; roughly 7 million children aged 5–17 remained out of school, and sustainability of free education was under discussion. | Social-policy implementation and limitation; 6B.1–6B.6, 6B.10, 8B.2, 8B.6–8B.7. | E017 | UNICEF/ECW: 2022 displacement and conflict severely constrained schooling; donor-supported programs worked with government and provincial authorities, but 3.2 million primary-age children were out of school. | Social outcomes, donor attribution, implementation constraints; 6B.2–6B.6, 6B.10, 8B.6–8B.10. ([source](https://www.unicef.org/drcongo/en/press-releases/call-substantial-increase-funding-crisis-affected-children)) |
| E018 | BTI: Tshisekedi’s 100-day program and national development plan identified infrastructure, health, education, governance, peace, and growth priorities, but implementation was weak and constrained by personnel, budgets, coalition politics, and donor dependence. | Program/implementation evidence, partly retrospective; 5B.1–5B.7, 6B.1–6B.6, 8B.1–8B.10. Allegation and associate conduct must remain distinct from Tshisekedi personally. ([source](https://www.presidence.cd/actualiteune-detail/actualite/reforme_de_la_justice_pres_de_80_hauts_magistrats_ont_prete_serment_devant_le_president_de_la_republique)) |
| E022 | Presidency: national cybersecurity strategy 2022–25 was developed under the presidential cabinet, validated by Tshisekedi, and presented to cabinet in October 2022. | Direct target-period program/action; 1B.8–1B.10, 4B.9, 8B.1–8B.6. The source establishes adoption, not implementation or effectiveness. ([source](https://www.presidence.cd/actualite-detail/actualite/la_strategie_nationale_de_cybersecurite)) |
| E023 | IAEA legal factsheet: DRC has NPT safeguards in force and an Additional Protocol signed in 2003. | Nuclear/legal exposure context only; 1B.1–1B.10. It does not establish a Tshisekedi-period nuclear decision. ([source](https://ola.iaea.org/Applications/FactSheets/Country/Detail?code=CD)) |

## Chapter yield

- **1B:** Local package had no retained nuclear/existential-risk facts. Web research found standing nonproliferation safeguards and a 2022 cybersecurity strategy, but no accepted evidence of nuclear weapons authority, nuclear coercion, proliferation, or catastrophic-risk use. Cyber governance is the only plausible target-period exposure found.
- **2B:** Strongest chapter yield. Local UCDP/SIPRI facts establish conflict exposure and military burden, not personal responsibility. E005 and E010–E013 add attribution-separated armed-conflict, regional diplomacy, threat-justification, and peace-process evidence.
- **3B:** Strong target-period evidence concerning state-of-siege restrictions, military courts, arrests, protests, journalists, and civilian harm. Attribution is strongest for national policy and state forces; nonstate and decentralized abuses remain separately identified.
- **4B:** Evidence covers CENI design, gubernatorial elections, vote-buying allegations, media restrictions, assembly restrictions, and internet conditions. The electoral evidence is mixed and includes both peaceful elections/observer access and institutional-independence concerns.
- **5B:** IMF provides the clearest ruler-period policy and implementation evidence. Mining-governance evidence adds a direct state-policy and audit trail but not personal enrichment. Local macro outcomes remain country context.
- **6B:** Education policy and implementation evidence is usable, but donor dependence, conflict, displacement, access gaps, and quality/sustainability limits are material. Local health and HDI facts remain outcome context.
- **7B:** Personal-nexus evidence is thin. The Gécamines audit and adviser episode are relevant but do not establish personal benefit by Tshisekedi. Official judicial and anticorruption actions are evidence of action, not proof of integrity or independence.
- **8B:** Program evidence exists for infrastructure, education, macroeconomic reform, cybersecurity, judicial reform, and regional security diplomacy. Implementation is mixed and constrained; BTI is useful but partly retrospective and not a substitute for 2022 administrative records.

## Source diversity and reuse

Source families include the DRC presidency and constitutional records, AU, EAC, UN, IMF, UNICEF/ECW, Amnesty, U.S. State Department/UNJHRO reporting, Freedom House, BTI, OCCRP, Reuters, and IAEA.

Same-URL splits were retained where one source supported materially distinct claims:

- U.S. State Department: expression, assembly, elections, armed-group/FARDC conduct, displacement, and internet conditions.
- Amnesty: state-of-siege purpose, civilian-harm trend, activist detention, military courts, and protest violence.
- IMF: macro policy, reform implementation, inflation, growth, and structural-governance conditions.
- UNICEF/ECW: education access, displacement, donor implementation, and remaining coverage gaps.

Cross-chapter reuse is explicit in the ledger rather than duplicated: state-of-siege evidence informs 2B, 3B, 4B, and 8B; IMF reform evidence informs 5B and 8B; education evidence informs 6B and 8B; the Gécamines audit informs 5B, 7B, and 8B with different attribution limits.

## Rejected or downgraded candidates

- Supplied local country indicators were retained as structured context, never as direct ruler evidence.
- Freedom House 2022 material was treated mainly as 2021 inherited/baseline evidence.
- Official presidential claims about Rwanda/M23 were retained as official claims, not independently verified facts.
- Official claims of judicial reform, free education, and cybersecurity progress establish actions or stated intent but not effectiveness, independence, or outcomes.
- Reuters/OCCRP adviser allegations remain allegations; the adviser’s conduct was not personalized to Tshisekedi.
- BTI’s 2022 report contains substantial 2019–2021 material and was used for program, constraint, and implementation context rather than as a pure 2022 outcome record.
- Search-result snippets, Wikipedia, generic organization pages, and unlocated homepage material were excluded from defensible evidence.

## Main unresolved gaps

- No ruler-specific 2022 evidence was found for nuclear weapons, nuclear coercion, proliferation, or biological/chemical weapons governance.
- M23/Rwanda attribution, FARDC command responsibility, civilian-protection performance, and accountability require further primary UN Group of Experts, MONUSCO, court, and military-record review.
- CENI independence, opposition treatment, media pressure, and 2022 electoral preparation need direct CENI, parliamentary, church-observer, and civil-society records.
- Free-education implementation needs budget execution, teacher-payment, enrollment, attendance, quality, and audit evidence.
- Economic policy needs 2022 budget execution, distributional/household evidence, and independent assessment of mining-contract reforms.
- Personal integrity requires direct evidence concerning Tshisekedi’s own disclosures, conflicts, decisions, correction behavior, and response to scrutiny.
- Effectiveness requires a fuller program-to-implementation chain with dated administrative outputs and independently measured results.

## Local-data audit

Retained as context: all supplied 2B local facts LF001–LF112; 3B facts LF113–LF333; 4B facts LF334–LF640; 5B facts LF641–LF777; 6B facts LF778–LF837; 7B facts LF838–LF893; and 8B facts LF894–LF921, subject to their supplied warnings and period roles.

Used most directly as target-period context: UCDP 2022 conflict/fatality fields, SIPRI 2022 military-spending fields, 2022 physical-integrity and political-freedom indicators, 2022 macroeconomic outcomes, 2022 HDI/service outcomes, WGI/V-Dem corruption and capacity fields.

Left unused as evidentiary support for ruler conduct: all country-level facts lacking a direct ruler-action or attribution pathway; all empty 1B local mappings; all local facts explicitly marked as inherited, aggregate, or insufficient for attribution.

## Updated 1B assessment

The previous “no evidence” conclusion was not saturated. This pass added:

- A target-period nuclear-risk reduction/legal action: TPNW ratification.
- Independent nuclear safeguards reporting.
- Independent chemical-weapons treaty and implementation records.
- A target-period cybercrime legislative request.
- Cybersecurity strategy adoption and legislative progression.
- Independent financial-sector cybersecurity oversight evidence showing implementation gaps.
- Specialist-source searches for biological weapons, chemical weapons, nuclear safeguards, and CBRN governance.

The remaining gap is substantive rather than merely bibliographic: I found no traceable 2022 evidence of Tshisekedi directing, expanding, concealing, or recklessly managing nuclear, biological, or chemical weapons capabilities. The safeguards and treaty records establish legal posture and external monitoring, not personal command behavior.

Cybersecurity now has both official action evidence and independent implementation-gap evidence. However, the available material concerns digital governance and financial-sector cyber oversight; it does not establish governance of catastrophic cyber operations, offensive cyber capability, or classified command systems.

## Search coverage and saturation

I used separate English and French searches across:

- nuclear safeguards, NPT, TPNW, IAEA safeguards conclusions;
- CWC, OPCW Article VII, biological-weapons implementation, CBRN safety;
- cyber strategy, cybercrime law, national cybersecurity agency, digital code;
- adverse/contrary queries concerning implementation gaps and secrecy;
- specialist and institutional sources including IAEA, UNODA, OPCW, IMF, national legislative reporting, and French-language local reporting.

The nuclear and chemical treaty/status search is now reasonably saturated for publicly accessible 2022 records. It still cannot rule out classified or undisclosed activity. Biological-weapons governance remains less well documented: no DRC-specific 2022 implementation, confidence-building, inspection, or adverse record was located.

--- SUPERVISOR RESEARCH TAKEOVER ROUND 2 ---

Local repository access remained blocked by the sandbox (`bwrap: loopback: Failed RTM_NEWADDR`), so I could not read or update `research-materials.md` or `research-ledger-manifest.json`. | E038 | After M23 attacks and the seizure of Kiwanja and Rutshuru, DRC authorities accused Rwanda of support, expelled Rwanda’s ambassador, restricted entry, and reinforced FARDC while reiterating commitment to Nairobi and Luanda processes. | MONUSCO Secretary-General report S/2022/892, paras. 5–6, pp. 2–3. | Direct state-response evidence; accusation remains an official claim, not independently closed. `2B.1, 2B.3, 2B.6, 2B.7, 8B.3` ([report](https://monusco.unmissions.org/sites/default/files/sg_report_-_english_s-2022-892.pdf)) |
| E039 | Tshisekedi called on Congolese youth to enlist in the army and encouraged vigilante groups, while warning of sanctions for xenophobia and hate speech against Rwandophone communities. | S/2022/892, paras. 6–7, p. 2. | Direct presidential statement and policy signal; does not establish operational results. `2B.3, 2B.6, 2B.9, 3B.7, 8B.3` ([report](https://monusco.unmissions.org/sites/default/files/sg_report_-_english_s-2022-892.pdf)) |
| E040 | The 23 November Luanda mini-summit attended by Tshisekedi agreed on a cessation-of-hostilities timetable, M23 withdrawal from occupied localities, and coordination with the Nairobi process. | S/2022/892, para. 12, pp. 3–4. | Direct diplomatic-process evidence; agreement does not prove durable compliance. `2B.1, 2B.7, 2B.10, 8B.3, 8B.9` ([report](https://monusco.unmissions.org/sites/default/files/sg_report_-_english_s-2022-892.pdf)) |

| E042 | From September–October 2022, MONUSCO support contributed to trials and convictions of at least three FARDC soldiers, one police officer, and 16 armed-group members for human-rights violations. | S/2022/892, para. 31, p. 6. | Target-period accountability evidence and contrary evidence to total impunity; scope and independence remain limited. `2B.8, 3B.5, 3B.10, 7B.7, 8B.4` ([report](https://monusco.unmissions.org/sites/default/files/sg_report_-_english_s-2022-892.pdf)) |
| E043 | By November 2022, DRC had approximately 5.7 million internally displaced people; about 1.5 million had been displaced during 2022, mainly by armed attacks and clashes, with roughly 280,000 newly displaced in Rutshuru after renewed M23/FARDC fighting. | S/2022/892, paras. 32–33, pp. 6–7. | Exposure and outcome evidence; not independently attributable to one ruler or actor. `2B.9, 2B.10, 6B.2, 6B.5, 8B.7, 8B.10` ([report](https://monusco.unmissions.org/sites/default/files/sg_report_-_english_s-2022-892.pdf)) |
| E044 | The Church of Christ in Congo said CENI had exceeded the operational periods in its February 2022 roadmap and urged publication of the full electoral calendar, criticizing the process as insufficiently transparent. | Actualité.cd, 28 July 2022. | Domestic religious-observer criticism; institutional concern, not proof of presidential interference. `4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.8, 4B.10` ([source](https://actualite.cd/index.php/2022/07/28/rdc-lecc-note-le-depassement-des-periodes-operatoires-presentees-dans-la-feuille-de)) |
| E045 | CENI published the electoral calendar for the 2022–2027 cycle in November 2022, establishing a dated administrative preparation milestone for the 2023 elections. | Radio Okapi report, 24 Nov. 2022. | Direct electoral-administration evidence; publication alone does not establish fairness or readiness. `4B.1, 4B.4, 4B.5, 8B.3, 8B.4` ([source](https://www.radiookapi.net/node/232990)) |

### Chapter-by-chapter handoff

- **1B:** Recovered one target-period CBRN governance unit, but no DRC-specific biological-weapons implementation or catastrophic cyber/classified-system evidence. The biological-weapons gap remains a genuine shortfall, not negative evidence.
- **2B:** Added primary UN evidence on M23 escalation, presidential mobilization, diplomacy, ceasefire arrangements, displacement, and accountability. M23/Rwanda attribution and durable ceasefire compliance remain unresolved.
- **3B:** Added UN documentation of protest repression, ANR detention, democratic-space deterioration, and prosecutions of security personnel. Geographic coverage and command responsibility remain incomplete.
- **4B:** Added domestic church criticism of CENI delays and the official 2022–2027 calendar. No direct evidence yet links Tshisekedi personally to CENI implementation or correction decisions.
- **5B:** Added the budget-governance decree and IMF budget-execution discrepancies. Fiscal reform action exists, but execution, distributional effects, procurement, and mining-revenue tracing remain incomplete.
- **6B:** Added World Bank evidence on enrollment, payroll, grievance redress, teacher conduct, and donor-supported delivery, balanced by UNICEF evidence of school closures and displacement. Quality, attendance, and national budget execution remain unresolved.
- **7B:** Added provisional arrest and acquittal records for the adviser. This materially improves disposition and contrary evidence, but the required personal-integrity nexus for Tshisekedi remains unproven.
- **8B:** Added cross-program implementation and failure evidence for security, elections, fiscal governance, education, and accountability. The main remaining gap is dated administrative outcomes that separate presidential responsibility from donors, shared executive authority, inherited weakness, and conflict shocks.

| ID | Source-claim unit and locator | Attribution, contrary evidence, mappings |
|---|---|---|
| **E054** | On 30 August 2022, DRC’s CBRN program described national capacity-building for chemical, biological, radiological, and nuclear risks, including updating the national action plan and training experts. [Actualité.cd, paras. 109–116](https://actualite.cd/2022/08/30/la-rdc-continue-se-preparer-pour-lutter-contre-les-risques-chimiques-biologiques) | Target-period institutional risk governance; no evidence of weapons possession or personal presidential direction. The program was EU/UNICRI-supported and partly inherited from 2012. **1B.5, 1B.6, 1B.9** |
| **E055** | The UN Secretary-General’s spring 2022 report recorded 1,233 human-rights violations and abuses in March–April, with armed groups responsible for 57%; it also documented convictions of FARDC soldiers, police officers, intelligence agents, and armed-group members. [S/2022/503, paras. 25–29](https://documents.un.org/api/symbol/access?l=en&s=S%2F2022%2F503&t=pdf) | Independent UN evidence separates nonstate violence from state-force accountability. It does not establish Tshisekedi’s personal direction or knowledge. **2B.8, 2B.9, 3B.5, 3B.9, 8B.4** |
| **E056** | A GEC/Ebuteli/Berci poll conducted in August 2022 found only 28% favorable opinion of CENI president Denis Kadima; the report also records opposition distrust of CENI and concerns about judicial independence. [Ebuteli, lines 56–64](https://www.ebuteli.org/publications/blogs/les-elections-en-rdc-une-liberte-trompeuse) | Target-period public-opinion and institutional-trust evidence. The article is partly retrospective and presents concerns, not adjudicated proof of presidential interference. **4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.8, 8B.4** |
| **E057** | CENCO and ECC trained at least 600 election observers on 23–24 December 2022 to monitor voter registration, CENI staff qualifications, political context, and process effectiveness. [7sur7.cd, paras. 30–49](https://7sur7.cd/index.php/2022/12/26/rdc-la-cenco-et-lecc-forment-des-observateurs-pour-sassurer-du-bon-deroulement-du) | Direct domestic-observer preparation and contrary oversight evidence. It demonstrates scrutiny capacity, not fair administration or presidential correction. **4B.5, 4B.8, 4B.10, 8B.3, 8B.4** |

[World Bank, paras. 148–157](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2022/06/28/world-bank-approves-750-million-to-support-critical-governance-reforms-transport-infrastructure-and-digital-connectivity) | Target-period government program under shared executive and donor authority. [World Bank, paras. 46–54](https://blogs.worldbank.org/en/nasikiliza/free-primary-schooling-drc-progress-despite-perils) | Mixed implementation evidence with donor verification and explicit limitations. It cannot establish nationwide quality, attendance, or sole presidential responsibility. **6B.1, 6B.3, 6B.4, 6B.6, 6B.7, 8B.2, 8B.6, 8B.8** |

It concerns institutional conditions and does not establish Tshisekedi’s ownership, benefit, knowledge, or tolerance. **5B.5, 5B.7, 7B.3, 7B.6, 7B.7, 8B.6** |

### Chapter handoff

- **1B:** CBRN searches produced E054, but no DRC-specific 2022 biological-weapons declaration, inspection record, or confidence-building submission. Classified/offensive cyber governance remains unobservable. This is a documented shortfall, not evidence of absence.

- **2B:** Primary UN material strengthened the record on conflict exposure, state-force violations, accountability, and nonstate attribution. M23/Rwanda attribution, FARDC command responsibility, and durable ceasefire compliance remain unresolved.

- **3B:** The UN spring report adds earlier-period abuse and accountability evidence. Geographic coverage, detention records, local-language documentation, and command-level responsibility remain incomplete.

- **4B:** E056 and E057 materially improve domestic-observer and institutional-trust coverage. They remain insufficient to establish presidential control over CENI, judicial independence, opposition treatment, or correction behavior.

- **5B:** E058 adds a dated governance, infrastructure, and connectivity implementation opportunity. E060 adds independent beneficial-ownership and extractive-sector transparency gaps. Budget execution, procurement outcomes, household distribution, and mining-revenue tracing remain incomplete.

- **6B:** E059 strengthens the implementation-to-audit chain, while preserving donor attribution and insecurity constraints. Attendance, learning quality, provincial execution, and sustainability remain unresolved.

- **7B:** E060 adds an independent institutional transparency gap but no personal nexus. The adviser’s arrest/acquittal evidence remains disposition evidence about the adviser, not proof of Tshisekedi’s knowledge or benefit.

- **8B:** The new records improve cross-program coverage across CBRN governance, security accountability, electoral oversight, economic governance, education delivery, and extractive transparency. Dated outcome evidence and counterfactual separation from donors, inherited weakness, shared authority, and conflict shocks remain limited.

### Manifest changes required
